i-d8f60b2d6de9cced-1	Mr. Old President! The labour market is perhaps Sweden's largest policy area, and in eight minutes it is not possible to cover all parts. Later in the debate, my colleagues Saila Quicklund and AnnAnn-Sofie Lifvenhage will talk about everything from work environment to matching and unemployment insurance. Mr. Old President! The rising unemployment rate is Sweden's biggest economic and social problem. Above all, of course, it is for the individual who loses his job and has less opportunity for self-sufficiency. But this also applies to society as a whole. Unemployment not only makes it difficult for welfare gains when fewer people pay taxes and more tax money goes to benefits, but it also leads in the long run to poorer pensions. It makes integration impossible for people who are trapped in lifelong exclusion. This, in turn, leads to the hopelessness and frustration that, among other things, paves the way for the recruitment of criminal gangs. Repressing unemployment is crucial for Sweden's future. However, the Corona crisis is only a minor part of the explanation of Sweden's economic problems. The fact is that even before the crisis we had the lowest growth per person in the whole of Europe. Despite Stefan Lövven's promise that Sweden would have the EU's lowest unemployment rate, we already had the EU's fifth highest before the crisis. Sweden was also the only country where unemployment increased between autumn 2014 and until the outbreak of the Corona crisis. Mr. Old President! When representatives of the parties to the January Agreement – those who have presented the budget that we are now discussing – try to make it as if Sweden is on the right track and that it is moving in the right direction, they always start from March 2020. However, it should be remembered that using March 2020 as a starting point, all curves will look good, regardless of the area. The problem with the budget from the January agreement parties that we are now discussing is that there is no common strategy on how to solve Sweden's problems. You don't have a whole, you don't have a red thread and you don't even have a common picture of the problems. Even less do you have the solutions. Only the widely differing wish lists of four parties have been compiled. With a budget of 105 billion, you can push everyone's wishes into it. However, despite 105 billion – the largest ever – there is, in principle, a complete lack of measures to increase jobs and employment. On the other hand, Mr President-in-Office, there are plenty of expensive reforms that reduce employment. That is the last thing Sweden needs. The Centre Party's representatives are talking with a loud voice about the reform of labour law, but we see that the Centre Party's promises in this regard run out into the sand, are being eroded and are now being investigated. Mr. Old President! Never before have the conditions for groups that are far from the labour market been as difficult even before a crisis struck. Despite a prolonged and prolonged boom, the government did not succeed in breaking the great alienation and dependency on grants that many people in Sweden have been in for a long time. The crisis has hit Sweden hard, and the high level of unemployment is at risk of getting stuck at high levels. We now have mass unemployment among many groups. That's what it looks like, Mr. Old President. Of the more than 100 000 people who have been given notice, a quarter have so far become unemployed, which is already a higher figure compared to the financial crisis, when one fifth of the respondents became unemployed. The difference in unemployment between immigrants and Swedish-born people has never been greater, and above all immigrant women are being knocked out of the labour market at a rapid pace. Youth unemployment is the highest in 100 years, and unemployment among immigrants in Sweden is among the highest in the EU. Mr. Old President! The Moderate plan to break unemployment is therefore on two legs. These are both economic measures to repress unemployment during the Corona crisis and structural reforms to solve Sweden's problems of exclusion and low growth. In order to relaunch the economy, reforms are needed for growth, especially in the labour market. These include lower thresholds, more access to the labour market, reform of labour law, increased mobility, better conditions for transition throughout the world of work, a reformed employment service and an unemployment fund that more clearly encourages job search, to name but a few. Mr. Old President! The Moderates also want to continue to lower taxes for those who work. We are doing this because it is right and because it provides more jobs and increases employment. We're doing it too because it's fair. There's a lot of pressure to pay off. We want to reduce taxes for everyone who works but with a focus on those with low and medium-sized incomes. We also reduce marginal tax rates for most people with ordinary occupations, such as nannys, nurses and bus drivers. So it becomes more profitable for them to work for an extra hour or to go up to full time. The strengthening of the employment tax credit thus gives us more hands and more money in welfare. Together with a benefit reform with, among other things, a premium ceiling, lower taxes on low and medium-sized incomes are crucial to breaking unemployment. We also want to demand that those who have immigrated to Sweden gradually qualify for Swedish benefit systems and start earning them. Mr. Old President! Let me conclude by stating that the Moderates are a party for those who work, for those who want to work and for those who have worked.

i-d8f60b2d6de9cced-33	Mr. Old President! We are here today to debate two reports, and I would like to begin by clarifying our requests. In the AU2, we are in favour of the Swedish Democrats' reservation, and in the AU5, we support all the reservations of the Swedish Democrats, but we are only in favour of reservation 4. Mr. Old President! As far as the labour market situation is concerned, we note that the covid-19 pandemic has affected all parts of society, not least the labour market. Some sectors have been hit harder than others, and well over 100,000 people have been alerted to dismissal since the start of the pandemic. We note that certain necessary measures have been implemented through amending budgets but that further measures are needed to address the far-reaching problems for the economy in general and the labour market in particular. Unlike the government, with its efforts at ineffective measures and symbolism policies, we want to see investments in things that really stimulate purchasing power, job creation and growth. Mr. Old President! Sweden has major social problems that can be explained at least in part by large groups being outside the regular labour market linked to years of irresponsible immigration policy. In many cases it applies to people who actually have the potential to work. Here we can see that the government's policy has failed completely because the figures were alarming long before the pandemic started. The Swedish democrats' labour market policy is therefore based on pragmatic foundations and does not follow dogmas in the form of classic right and left-wing politics. We note that the Swedish model has proved successful in regulating the conditions between workers and larger companies. But we want to see greater flexibility and better conditions for small and medium-sized enterprises combined with a safe working environment. We believe that is where the jobs of the future will be created. However, much of the Swedish labour market and action policy must be rejected for two reasons. Firstly, it is spectacularly costly, and secondly, it is extremely inefficient. The inevitable conclusion is that labour market policy must be subject to reforms that involve less money for labour market measures and better conditions for smaller companies in particular. The incentives to create real jobs need to be increased. This means that the Swedish Democrats reject new start-up jobs, start-up jobs and some parts of the support for career introduction programmes. These are costly measures that year after year have shown terrible results and that after the end of the program have led to few regular jobs. We cannot stand here and debate these issues without mentioning the reform of the Employment Service. Before the pandemic hit Sweden, Arbetsförmedlingen was at the beginning of a reform process. For many years, we have been calling for a reform of the Swedish Public Employment Service in order to allow more players in the matching business. The private operators to take on the matching task must be continuously evaluated and monitored. Cheating and abuse must be combated, and access to support in all parts of the country must be ensured. Our vision for reforming the Public Employment Service remains firm, but reform must take place in a responsible manner. There is now a situation where Arbetsförmedlingen has to deal with an increased number of job seekers. According to the latest forecast, the number of job seekers will continue to rise until early 2021. It is important to take account of the fact that our country is in a state of crisis and to be open to the fact that it may be necessary to review the pace of reform. Mr. Old President! Our draft budget prioritises the importance of strengthening unemployment insurance for all. For several years we have highlighted the benefits of nationalised unemployment insurance. The pandemic has highlighted several shortcomings in the current system. The exceptional length of processing time has meant that a large number of jobseekers have been left without the compensation to which they are entitled. The safety net of welfare should ensure financial security in the event of unemployment, as it does in the event of illness. The right way forward is therefore a nationalised, compulsory and tax-funded unemployment insurance. Mr. Old President! In order to further strengthen the financial security of employees, we further believe that the compensation levels for the first hundred days of unemployment should be increased from SEK 910 to SEK 1,200 per day. Unlike the committee, we are not satisfied with the government's proposal for temporary increases in this area, but we want a permanent increase. Increased financial security allows people to focus on job seekers instead of worrying about the basics of everyday life. Mr. Old President! We see a need for a more ambitious labour market policy than the government offers. It is unfortunate that the committee does not see this need because fatalities are increasing in our workplaces today. We therefore believe that the Swedish Work Environment Authority's supervision work is absolutely central to the work environment, and we want to see an increased number of workplace inspections. We also want to see a higher level of funding for the Agency for Safety and Health at Work. Increased resources enable the Agency to take more investigative assignments with a focus on the pandemic's health and safety effects. And unfortunately, Mr President-in-Office, today we can see that the number of shootings in society and the number of fatalities in our workplaces is at exactly the same level, and that must be seen as a failure for the government. This is a subject that we will come back to in a report and debate after Christmas.

We have seen hundreds of thousands of people go to unemployment once our society has been shut down. Even before the crisis, there was a serious situation in which the labour market was divided in depth. Today, they are becoming more vulnerable. Those who have been unemployed for a long time end up in the back of the line. The division of the tongue is further deepened. Digitisation and transition of the labour market have been involuntarily put on some kind of firm forward where jobs stop but are also created at an unprecedented rate. This is a time when Sweden's labour market really needs reforms, strengthening the chances of creating new jobs and finding new ways of finding employees for growing small businesses. It is therefore gratifying that there has been a drama on the positive side as well. Recently, on 4 December, the social partners agreed on a key historic agreement, a kind of Saltsjöbaden 2.0. For this agreement, which now comes in the time of the Count, we owe a great deal of thanks to those who choose to support, i.e. the Confederation of Swedish Enterprise, the official unions in PTK, IF Metall, Municipal and the majority of LO. They were able to conclude this historic new agreement, despite the fact that LO dropped off an agreement on which it shook hands. I am also proud that the Centre Party's long forty-year efforts to reform LAS, which for many decades neither bourgeois nor social-democratic governments were able to carry out, are now finally becoming a reality under the pressure of the January Agreement and the solid and professionally conducted state inquiry. For decades, the square rules in LAS – last in, first out – have put those who are weakest at the back of the queue and increased the division. Professor Lindbeck Lindbeck, the netor of Swedish economics, unfortunately recently deceased, made these questions the most important issue of his life. He said that for many decades Sweden had had insiders, who are on the inside of the labour market, and once they come out they become outsiders who are more and more vulnerable. Unfortunately, Lindbeck Assar must not see that these outsiders now have a greater chance of entering the labour market when the major and important reforms come into place. The rules become more flexible so that companies dare to take the risk, even in times like Corona, of hiring those they are more uncertain about without incurring bureaucratic costs. It will be cheaper for employers to do the right thing when they hire and more expensive when they do the wrong thing. We go back to a Swedish model, such as the one in Saltsjöbaden in 1938, where the parties themselves decide and we politicians do not put a wet blanket of regulations over and increase and make more complicated. Now that the parties have agreed, we can see how little substance scare propaganda has. As little as before, you can be fired in Sweden without objective reasons: not because you are pregnant, ill, pursuing your trade union mission or because of discrimination. Of course not! Protection against arbitrary dismissals remains. But anyone who harasses, grotesques or goes to work intoxicated and endangers his employees can no longer be protected by rules made for another time and which are sometimes, quite frankly, absurd. While we make the rules more flexible, the Saltsjöbad Agreement gives millions of Swedes the chance to further educate themselves, to try their wings and further develop their skills in life. A unique new study funding system is to be introduced, where anyone who has worked can study for up to one year with 80 percent replaced salary from the state and from the parties. Not only those in permanent employment, but also those with fixed periods of time get the chance to change just when we need this most. At this time of major transformation, Sweden is finally entering the 21st century into the labour market as well. We are also doing a lot more in the budget and the major reforms in the January agreement. It can be reminded. We have apprenticeship jobs called start-up jobs that the parties have agreed on, where you can get in on a little lower salary. We are introducing an intensive year for refugees, where passivity and waiting are replaced by fighting in the sweat of one's face to show what you can with intensive Swedish and intensive practice and then get a chance to really get into Swedish society. And, yes, we're reforming the Arbetsförmedlingen... albeit in a while, 2022. We do this because Sweden spends so much money, about 50 000 per unemployed person, on an employment agency that unfortunately gives so little. Despite many good intermediaries and despite many good local examples, it is generally only a small gutter that gets help. Therefore, we must learn from the best examples in the world. There you release the power of independent, non-profit and private intermediaries who get paid only for the lasting job results they really deliver – no parking in crafting. They are paid many times more to help those who have the heaviest and hardest way to work than those who have longer education, so that they give priority to those who are the toughest. You can scale up what works for the unemployed in the form of help and stop what takes both the time of the unemployed and the resources that could be used to help much better. At the same time, these intermediaries should be closely monitored and followed up. Here we have a long way to go, step by step, to make all this as accurate and as accurate as possible. We must also ensure that the Public Employment Service will continue to exist throughout the country. For many years, Arbetsförmedlingen has incomprehensiblely retreated from the countryside and centralised its activities without even saving money. Inhabitants of small rural municipalities have been allowed to travel hundreds of miles just to meet a case officer. Now we have finally started to turn this around. Offices have begun to open again and operations are re-starting, but that is certainly not enough. We notice on a daily basis that the Employment Service is not there in small rural municipalities. Therefore, in this budget, we will raise a quarter of a billion and in future regulatory letters we will tighten up the requirements further in order to stubbornly strengthen and secure the local presence until the Employment Service as a government agency is in place throughout the country and meets the unemployed in all municipalities. Already, the unemployed must face an effective, result-driven intermediary. At the beginning of the pandemic, in late April, Annika Qarlsson and I received an email from a man in Kungälv. He told us that he had been given notice in February in connection with the pandemic – a tragedy for him, of course. Only at the end of April did he receive a letter from Arbetsförmedlingen, where he was called to his first meeting with an administrator – hold on now! – at the beginning of December, in practice now that I am standing here in the speaker's chair. This isn't good enough. We cannot just keep letting the mills of bureaucracy grind in anticipation of reform. In the pandemic, we must have all hands on deck, and that is why we need as many results-based, efficient and driven intermediaries as we can get. Therefore, we are now ensuring that tens of thousands of unemployed people during the pandemic – more than ever before but still too few – face an effective, performance-based stand-alone intermediary who can really help them with training and jobs despite the fact that it is a very dark time in the labour market. In conclusion, I would just like to say that Sweden is a rich country with enormous conditions, even during the pandemic, but we have had a deep and serious division that created poverty, exclusion and segregation. With these reforms, not least with the hope of the new Saltsjöbad agreement, which is now lit for the whole labour market, we can finally start breaking the division – in the time of the Count, in the time of the pandemic – and start making the labour market whole again.

In our draft budget we spend the big money on welfare in order to create better quality, better working conditions and more colleagues for those who daily carry care, school and care. We spend about 70 billion more than the government on reforms aimed at building a robust, sustainable and fair society for all. And we do it with the same financial savings as the government in the last row. So we are financing our reforms with increased tax revenues that would be borne more fairly by high-income earners and by people with large wealth and capital income. Mr. Old President! Nevertheless, I would like to add, under the special circumstances, that the government has made a number of financial contributions and calls for action on the occasion of the pandemic which is laudable. The Left Party has supported most of them and has been the proposer of several of them. But in the ordinary budget we see that when the robustness of society is tested, the answer is unfortunately anything but satisfactory and far-sighted. As far as the labour market is concerned, we are undoubtedly in a trying time as a society. Unemployment has increased as a result of the pandemic. Particularly serious is the increase in long-term unemployment, which has causes that go further than the corona outbreak. But just when labour market policy would need to accumulate and function well, the January parties are in the process of ideologically unsound experimentation. Mr. Old President! Many may associate the Center Party with the extremely costly industrial policy failures of Postnord and Nuon – the fiasco that cost the public tens of billions – and that is understandable. Unfortunately, however, I believe that when we look back in a few years' time, the destruction of active labour market policy by the Center Party in general, and the State Employment Service in particular, will be able to be summed up into even greater losses for Swedish society. This is not a process that begins and ends with this year's budget. The Reinfeldt government has already converted large parts of Arbetsförmedlingen into a procurement body. This led to great and costly problems. But now that the need for a comprehensive state labour market policy with high-quality efforts is greater than it has been for a very long time, the January parties intend instead to shift the documented misconceptions on a full scale, despite warnings from virtually everyone with practical experience in labour market policy. Only a few weeks ago, the trade union ST, which organises many of those who actually do the job in labour market policy, presented a new report. It notes that the governance of the Employment Service has transferred resources from groups that need them most, the long-term unemployed, to those who are easy to find new jobs. It is therefore quite the opposite of effective priorities. Occupational groups with special skills to work with people who for various reasons are far from the labour market have had to get rid of or relocate to expand services to help people fill out forms and control them. It is nothing less than a destruction of capital. Now the municipalities are also panicked about the effects of this accident. There will be no employment agencies in more than 200 of Sweden's municipalities. SKR points out an increasingly urgent need for the state to assume its responsibilities and also recalls that labour market policy cannot be reduced to remote contacts. In addition to the costs of the job seekers who are under pressure, we thus get a passover of costs to the municipalities – costs that pressure things like school and care for the elderly. Something could have been done about this in the January parties' budget. But no, no calls from reality can interfere with this center party thought project that an S-led government is now undertaking. The reform of the Employment Service, as the dismantling is euphemistically called, will continue. Instead, the draft budget of the Left Party is based on real needs. We allocate 2 billion more to labour market policy interventions, and 1.5 billion more to the Public Employment Service's administrative appropriations – something that is necessary if you are actually to get the signs that the Minister for Labour talked about and if you are actually to be able to arrange the efforts that help people into working life. Mr. Old President! I will briefly address two more of the areas affected by the Left Party's draft budget. In our budget, we contribute more money to the Swedish Work Environment Agency and to the activities of regional safety representatives. These are important initiatives in a situation where we are still suffering during the severe cuts in inspection activities made under the Reinfeldt Government and see changes in the labour market that require more from both the Swedish Work Environment Authority's inspectors and regional safety representatives. Our efforts may be contrasted not only with the unwillingness to spend more budgetary resources, but also with the position of the cooperation parties to vote down, together with the right-wing opposition, a proposal to extend the possibilities for regional safety representatives to enter more precisely those workplaces where they are most needed. It is one of all the areas where the differences between the right and the left in politics are so acutely visible, i.e. how to value the people who do the work that is the source of all the wealth that in our society is so unequal and unjustly distributed. Finally, our proposals to combat cheating and exploitation linked to subsidised employment. Such employment can be very effective and lead to welfare gains for both the individual and the national economy as a whole. But we have seen for a long time that companies and businesses are making use of these subsidies. In some sectors it is a serious problem for the whole structure of the economy when it is put into systems by certain operators. The Left Party has therefore proposed, among other things, the following: an obligation should be imposed on employers who employ staff with subsidies to issue certificates of employment to employees when the employment ceases and a re-imposed requirement that non-wage employment benefits should also comply with or be equivalent to the relevant collective agreements in this area. We have also highlighted proposals to strengthen the role of trade unions in detecting and deterring the systematic exploitation of subsidised employment. These are demands which, of course, should be self-evident but which are therefore rejected by the committee. That is regrettable. Unfortunately, however, we will probably have reason to return to these problems in the coming years.

These have been measures to save lives and reduce the spread of infection. Despite extensive efforts to save jobs, unemployment has increased dramatically. In an increasingly rapidly changing labour market, it will become increasingly important for education and labour market policies to go hand in hand. The provision of skills and the development of skills are crucial to meeting the needs of the future labour market. It is therefore very welcome that the Government has given the Employment Service more opportunities to assign job seekers to regular education and that opportunities to study under both unemployment and short-term lay-off have been strengthened. The budget includes further extensions in adult education, polytechnics and higher education, as well as increased resources for more people to take part in labour market training. In this way, we are using the crisis to improve people's and companies' future opportunities for growth and development. The employment agency has increased resources, both to deal with the situation here and now and to strengthen the local presence and be a resource throughout the country. AF must be able to meet job seekers and employers locally, understand the local labour market and act as a strategic partner in relation to municipalities and other actors. At the same time, the reform process is continuing and additional resources are also being provided for this. A particularly welcome mission is that the Swedish Public Employment Service, in collaboration with other actors, will launch the intensive year for new arrivals, which aims to shorten the path to work, self-sufficiency and integration. Various actions such as language training, career guidance and validation need to be linked together to a faster and clearer chain for those who are in a position to enter the labour market faster. Another group that needs special attention is the approximately 12% of the population who have different forms of disability, of which about two thirds are considered to have a reduced working capacity. These people have an unemployment rate which is significantly higher than for the rest of the population. More people in this group also work part-time and have more time-limited employment. There is an untapped potential here that we should seize, both for the sake of society and for the benefit of the individual. Labour market policy needs to have a stronger focus on involving these people in working life. The Employment Service shall ensure competence for the proper functioning of support for these persons. Subsidised employment and other support is an important piece of the puzzle in this work, as is Samhall, which will create jobs for those who, due to their disability, are outside the labour market. In the wake of the pandemic, this group is in danger of moving a further step away from work, and that is why it is to be welcomed by the increase in the allocation of 400 million to Samhall. Another welcome initiative in the budget is the 30 million that is being added to the Swedish Work Environment Authority to strengthen and develop cooperation between authorities against working life crime. It is an important work The Swedish Work Environment Agency, the police, the Swedish Tax Agency, the Swedish Social Insurance Agency, the Swedish Migration Agency, the Swedish Equality Agency and the Swedish Economic Crime Agency do to combat tax offences, work environment crimes, misuse of subsidies and exploitation of vulnerable people, not least labour immigrants and other migrants, and to ensure that competition takes place on equal terms. During last year, nearly 2 000 checks were carried out, which resulted in approximately one in ten controlled companies having to close down all or part of their activities and more than 10 million fines being imposed. In a labour market that is changing at a rapid pace, and especially in an economic downturn, economic security on the way between jobs is an important element. During the first years of the alliance government, dramatic changes and deteriorations were made to the unemployment fund. It became more expensive to participate, lower compensation, more difficult to qualify and almost impossible to predict your insurance cover. As a result, almost half a million people left the unemployment fund. Then we know how it went: the crisis struck. During the S-led government, several improvements have been made to the unemployment fund, and now during the pandemic the rules have been changed so that more people receive compensation and both floors and ceilings have been raised. It is very welcome that these changes are now being extended to safeguard people's income while maintaining the purchasing power of the economy. Finally, I have chosen to focus on our proposals in this speech, although there is, of course, much to say about the opposition's proposals. It is notable, however, that both the Moderates, the Swedish Democrats and the Christian Democrats want to make substantial cuts in this area of expenditure, between 6 1⁄2 billion and about 10 billion, compared to the government and the cooperation parties, in a situation where the need for support for conversion is greater than perhaps ever before. It is also remarkable that both the Moderates and the Christian Democrats want to lower the levels of the unemployment fund in the midst of an ongoing pandemic. Those who would be affected are the unemployed, who have already taken a hard blow in Corona's tracks. The same Moderates who in April wanted to spend 100 billion on aid to crisis-sponsoring companies – this month – now want to cut nearly 10 billion on aid to crisis-stricken people. I note that many exciting things are happening in Swedish politics, but one thing is certain: When it comes to increasing insecurity for ordinary people, you can always trust the right hand. With that, Mr President, I would like to support the committee's proposal for a decision in the AU2 and AU5 reports. I would also like to take this opportunity to wish the President's Bureau, all the Members and the office staff a happy and healthy Christmas and a happy New Year.

It is a question of changing valuation to highly esteem this competence, rather than to sort people out at a fixed age. My former boss and principal Sten-Åke-Åke Domeij had certainly not intended to quit the day the birth certificate said that the retirement age was reached. No, I don't think anyone of us who had him in charge would have thought that that energy thing would do that. But in a system that is more for the system than for flexible and thinking people there is nothing but to quit and start your own business in order to be able to continue using their skills. But we also see a society where skills development and career changes are more the rule than the exception. This means that we must be prepared to learn and learn new things throughout our working lives. It's a way to keep interest and go up for some, but also a challenge for others. In order for more people to be able to change and constantly work to make themselves employable, the system needs to be changed so that, for example, you can get tax deductions for your own training costs and that society stimulates re-training. Overall, this increases the importance of flexible adult learning that places new demands on the transition. The state therefore needs to make it easier for both employers and individuals to invest in education through their part and responsibility in the social contract. The Swedish labour market is plagued by lack of mobility and outdated regulations. People have increasingly different job opportunities, and businesses find it difficult to get people with the right skills for different jobs Madam President, I would like The aim should be to create unemployment insurance that provides temporary financial security, dedratises short-term unemployment and enables effective job search. We must realise that the 21st century labour market cannot be controlled by regulations from the 1970s. Today's employment protection makes it harder for people who do not like or fit their workplace to move forward. In order to improve mobility and matching in the labour market, strengthen Swedish competitiveness and give more opportunities to have a job, the rotation rules should be reformed to a greater extent based on who has the best competence to carry out the task. Other parts of the Employment Protection Act should also be reviewed. For example, probationary contracts should be extended from six to twelve months in order to make more people dare to try hiring another employee, which can lower the threshold for jobs for both young and newcomers. For this to be possible, labour market matching needs to work. Today, the Swedish labour market is characterised by major matching problems. While more people than ever need help to enter the labour market, recruitment problems are considerable. Employers do not find the right skills. A well-functioning match, and the ability to give the unemployed the right input, will play a central role in how we can deal with that challenge in the future. The inefficiencies and low trust of employment services are not the whole cause of the matching problem, but it becomes clear that reform is needed. We want to see a structure in which more actors take overall responsibility for their job seekers and receive compensation according to how well they succeed in helping the job seeker into work. Quality must be the focus. Madam President, I would like We need an unemployment insurance policy that is clear, where in the beginning the individual has a great deal of freedom to seek ways to the next job and where the drive to be active in it itself is rewarded. Work and economy should always pay off. An insurance policy should be a short-term help when you are between jobs and not a lasting livelihood. The Moderates want to create the conditions for a modern labour market where people can change their careers, build on their skills to keep a job or make a career. With stronger economic encouragement and greater freedom, Sweden could have a labour market model that creates both growth and real security: it has several employers to choose from. It becomes clear that we need unemployment insurance worthy of the name. With the starting position Sweden is in now, we rather need a conversion insurance to meet the future labour market, a labour market where more people dare to change and more people come to their own. i-d8f60b2d6de9cced-351	Madam President, I would like In Sweden there are about 10,000 preschools, and just over half a million children attend these. The share of employees with a pre-primary qualification is approximately 40 per cent. Another 20% are licensed child care workers. Most units meet the requirement for at least one educated preschool teacher throughout preschool, but some municipalities have as low a level as 6 per cent educated children's nurses and 13 per cent certified preschool teachers. 263 of the country's preschools even lack full staff with some form of identification. These are numbers from the National Board of Education. The proportion of pre-school teachers differs between different types of principals and municipalities. A report from SKR, Swedish Municipalities and Regions, mentions that the distance to higher education institutions with pre-school education affects the ability to recruit staff because preschool teachers stay very close to the higher education institution or university where they have trained. There is a high shortage of trained staff in rural and sparsely populated areas, as well as in areas furthest geographically from the places where these training courses are carried out. Although much can be done digitally and remotely – we've learned that in the past year – the statistics speak clearly: This is a growing problem. I recently participated in a pre-school webinars organised by several members of the Education Committee, where these facts and statistics were discussed and highlighted. This development applies not only to qualified teachers in preschool, but also to primary school. Sweden should be a country where people can live and work and have children in preschool and school even outside the larger places. The lack of qualified preschool teachers and primary school teachers in rural and rural areas needs to be addressed. 194 of Sweden's 290 municipalities fall within these criteria, and about 25 per cent of Sweden's population lives in these areas. Potentially, then, one in four children may attend preschool or primary school in the very areas where there is a serious shortage of qualified and trained staff. Furthermore, it should also be mentioned that 14% of Sweden's graduates choose to take employment outside the education industry or as teachers and simply choose to work with something else. In order to start a job where we try to remedy the teacher shortage in preschool and school, the Swedish Democrats therefore propose that graduates who take up employment in rural areas annually should receive a deduction of 15 per cent on their student debt, which would mean that the entire student debt is paid off after about seven years. Both the Municipal Investigation and the Rural Committee highlight the write-down of study debts as an appropriate measure to try to improve the supply of skills in some parts of the country. This investment amounts to approximately SEK 90 million for 2021, and a fully implemented proposal would entail a cost of approximately SEK 600 million per year. It is an initiative that would benefit an estimated 400 000 children and pupils in preschool and primary school only in rural areas. If we add teachers in occupations and high school teachers, who are included in this effort, the number increases even more, and we are approaching half a million students – to quote the Teachers' Association: ..................................................... Madam President, I would like In order to provide incentives for students to train in professions where there is a shortage of trained staff in the labour market, the Swedish Democrats propose targeted support in the form of an increased study grant of SEK 1 000 extra per month during the study months. This applies to those students who graduate in a shortage area, which refers to one of the ten programmes where there is a greatest shortage of skilled labour in the labour market. According to Arbetsförmedlingen's forecasts, these professions include occupational therapists, midwives, civil engineers, recreational educators, health professionals and doctors, IT staff, police, psychologists, dentists, dental hygienists and dental technicians, and preschool teachers and teachers. The proportion of empty places on spring courses in these examples varies between 76 and 86 percent. In the worst cases, then, we are only training 14% of actual needs. A higher study grant of SEK 1 000 per month would probably attract more students to these programmes, which is also confirmed by the parliamentary investigation service. We therefore invest in our budget 580 million for 2021 for this purpose. Sweden is a fantastic country with a well-developed study support system that soon goes into its 102nd year since Hjalmar Oscar-Ahlberg-Ahlberg in 1919 received the first Swedish student loan – 1 500 SEK for a full year to be paid back within ten years. Since 1919 Sweden has developed, and 1965 laid the foundations for the system we have today, which is a well-developed study support system. It is one of the world's most generous to enable studies for people from all walks of life, regardless of the individual's unique conditions. We should be very proud of this while there are always elements that can be improved and further developed. It is a grant paid for a maximum of 50 weeks for full-time studies. Last year, 6 300 people were granted this aid. A majority chose to study vocational programmes such as health and social care, children and leisure and vehicles and transport, i.e. in short-term occupations – and only 5% discontinued their studies. That is why we in Sweden have compulsory health insurance that we fund together and where we say that those who fall outside have a safety net that catches up. It becomes increasingly clear when we are in the midst of a pandemic, a pandemic where none of us know whether our life or health is in danger. But we're in danger because no one's immune and there's no vaccine. We know that we have large groups in our society that are extra vulnerable, and if the virus hits them, their lives may be in absolute danger. Just look at all our hospitals and the death tolls that are rising more and more every day. Madam President, I would like During this year, our committee, together with the government, with which we have good cooperation on many issues, has taken the initiative, among other things, to extend the remuneration of risk groups, i.e. workers who risk their lives if they go to work. It was decided that instead they would receive compensation and be able to stay at home and not put their lives at risk. And parents who have a child at risk should not be forced to work but be able to stay at home and take care of the child until the virus has been combated. Madam President, I would like It worked very well at the beginning of the pandemic. The Government made a proposal to extend the remuneration of risk groups for 90 days. When there were four days left, the government woke up and extended the compensation, on the initiative of the committee. This happened not only once, but twice, that is, the government was slow. According to the Swedish National Audit Office's criticism of how the government governs its agencies, there is no clear follow-up, and this is also reflected in these issues. The Moderates came forward with a committee initiative to grant an exemption for the examination of work capacity after 180 days for those who have suffered from covid-19, because we know very little about it. We know how to treat hernias and knee problems, but covid-19 we know very little about. There are many in our society who have suffered very seriously as a result of covid-19. It may be a person who has been in a respirator for 30 - 40 days, who has lost all weight and has not touched the body or anything. The person probably has the ability to work in his or her normal place of work, but recovery takes time. As we know very little about the disease and that the health care system is under pressure, we went forward with an initiative to extend it. The government made a proposal on the day the initiative was to be discussed, but if you are responsible for a country and have a whole department behind you, you should always be at the forefront. But enough about that. What we Moderates also want is for the government to develop insurance medicine decision-making documents for mental diagnoses. We know very much about the treatment of traditional diseases, which I mentioned – knee problems, back problems, shoulder problems etcetera – but we see that the number of sick leave due to psychosocial ill health is increasing. Today, there is no policy-making basis on which both the administrator at Försäkringskassan and the doctor can base themselves, so that at least one tries to work in a uniform manner. Today it is a bit like a lottery when you visit the doctor or when you come into contact with the Social Insurance Agency. It can be absolutely crucial how a medical opinion is written or what commitment there is in the case. That is why it is incredibly important to develop an insurance medical decision-making basis for mental diagnoses, because once people have been on sick leave, the principle and goal should be that as many people as possible should return to work. If there's anything we see in the coronapademine, it's the consequence of being isolated and shielded from social contacts. Sickness insurance and sickness benefit should go to the person in need, and there are and will be groups that will not be able to return to normal work or to the labour market at all. But the ambition should be that as many as possible should come back to some form of work. It is about the view of man and of human dignity. More and more people are getting mental diagnoses, and what we know is that social contact is extremely important. It is only to look at our elderly and what they get to go through at our retirement homes when they are cut off from the rest of society. Health insurance must not become an insurance policy that sends people into long-term exclusion, as it did in the early 2000s. Enough about that, Madam President. The Moderates' draft budget is a coherent unit, and since the Parliament in step one – the Framework Decision – has supported the government's framework for the state budget and the orientation of budgetary policy, we are not taking part in the current decision. Instead, we refer to our particular opinion.

We also believe that a different policy from that which has been pursued would have made it possible to cut the long-term care queues. It would also reduce the anxiety and stress of loved ones about being able to receive care on time. With a Christian-democratic policy, where we place emphasis on increasing people's empowerment and freedom of choice, better conditions are created for good health in society. Human dignity is not linked to the number of hours a person works, nor to how productive he or she is. We do not live to work, but a job can be crucial to a person's well-being, self-confidence and self-experience. Basically, man is industrious. We feel good to occupy ourselves and feel involved in the common. This can be done through work, as well as in family, family, and friends. Through a job, a person can create many of the conditions needed to feel involved. It cannot be ignored how important it is to have the opportunity to go to a job. I also think of all the small children who may not have the opportunity to see Mom and Dad go to a job. It makes me a little sad. We know that exclusion and vulnerability can often be inherited. So having a preventive perspective, which I started with, is important, as is an effective rehabilitation work to enable people to return to work. Madam President, I would like A politician should always be humble. A politician must never be so happy in a system that you do not see the problems that can actually appear. There is, I mean, no system that is absolutely perfect and fits everyone to one hundred percent. Of course, this is also true of the rehabilitation chain. The Christian Democrats therefore want to develop the rehabilitation system. We mean that not everyone can work 100 percent of full-time but that everyone should have the opportunity to work 100 percent of their ability. If work capacity, not incapacity for work, is at the centre and is the starting point, more flexibility is needed. That is why we Christian Democrats believe, for example, that we must change the fixed steps in sickness benefit, sickness benefit and rehabilitation allowance of 25, 50, 75 and 100 %. It has to be more flexible. A person may need to go up another 10 percent and can do so in a rehabilitation process. This is being denied to this person today, because the steps are set at 25 % intervals. In order to minimise the risk of things falling between the seats, we also want the Social Insurance Agency to have a much clearer mandate, for example to be responsible for conversion meetings with the Employment Service, which we know to be important. We want to give the insured person the right to something we call a mentor. Those who are in a vulnerable life situation or for other reasons are unable to assimilate a rehabilitation process may need someone at their side as help, sounding board and inspirational – an encouraging hand that can help people to step above a threshold they are unable to step over on their own. A mentor at his side also reduces the risk of a person falling apart. This contributes to reduced stress and anxiety. Take, for example, the Pension Agency's calculations, which show that an asylum seeker who is granted a residence permit represents a net cost to the pension system of SEK 800 000! If we can reduce asylum-related migration by a few thousand people, it is thus a saving of billions for Swedish taxpayers. Or take the Entrepreneurship Forum's report on self-sufficiency, which shows that it takes on average twelve years for foreigners to become self-sufficient in Sweden. A judgment of the Migration Court of Appeal stated that a large proportion of those under 18 were actually to be assessed as an alternative need for protection and a residence permit. However, the long processing times allowed many to reach 18 before the asylum decision was taken. Others were recorded in age, either by administrators or by highly criticized medical age assessments. Then the circle was closed. Through this Kafka-like situation, children have been deprived of their right of asylum. The fact that they are now adults does not make this injustice any less serious. There is a political responsibility in this which weighs heavily and which cannot be escaped by constantly repeating various lies about the group that has suffered the injustice. That is why the Left Party has long wanted to see a comprehensive solution with permanent residence permits for the unaccompanied. It would have been the best, easiest and most realistic – both for the children and young people who have been affected by the unreasonable trips I have mentioned and for our society. The fact that new groups are constantly excluded from society's safety net does not make it any better. They become easy targets for exploitation as cheap labour or for being involved in criminal activities. This should also be understood by parties who are constantly talking about shadow society. Unfortunately, it was not as the Left Party had wished. Instead, some young people would be given a new opportunity through the new High School Act. But the law was so poorly constructed that it was obvious that injustice, difficult interpretations, and the risk of exploitation and vulnerability remained. The government was not interested in remedying these shortcomings. Here we are again. The Pandemin makes it difficult for the first hill students, who against all odds have made their way through the upper secondary school law's pretzel hooks and irritants, to find work. While the government and parliament have acted quickly in a wide range of areas to ensure that people are not unduly affected by the consequences of the pandemic, the government has delayed finding solutions for people in the area of migration. Once such proposals were presented, the Center Party chose to turn its back on the young. The young people, Madam President, are part of Sweden, and Sweden is part of them. They've invested in us and we've invested in them. It is high time to realise that their situation needs to be normalized and to replace the patchwork of insecurity they have encountered with long-term and security. In the long run, Madam President, after their education, they are an important part of the generational change that must now take place as the population ages rapidly and, not least, welfare is facing a huge shortage of labour. That is why the Left Party has budgeted for permanent residence permits for these young people. Our investment involves approximately 11,000 people. Based on estimates from the Parliamentary Investigation Service, this would cost 900 million. This would lead in the short term to savings by the Migration Board and the courts and to costs being moved to the expenditure area for the establishment of new arrivals. This is a recognition that we in this House have responsibilities when people get caught, not least when it comes to children and young people. This means redress for those who have been deprived of their right to asylum and a way into society rather than a naive exclusion. The young will not disappear. It is right and realistic to give them a secure future.

But the first thing you can see is that when you get this kind of legislation in place, you need to have it in a functional environment. Therefore, an effective and legally secure process where processing times are short is one of the prerequisites. We now see in the performance report that is also included in the budget how the time for a decision is shorter. That is good, even if there is a bit left before the situation is satisfactory. It facilitates both the integration of those who have been granted a residence permit and the return of those who are refused. That is why, once again, it is important to make the additions to the courts that are now being made. Another functional environment that Swedish migration policy needs to be in is the EU's way of addressing the issue together and the regulatory framework that governs. In this autumn, the Commission presented a new proposal which has now begun to be debated. I believe, even on the basis of our discussions in the Social Security Committee, that this is not really the proposal that any one of us would have liked to see. However, it is a proposal which may be in a position to go through and which could also allow a more integrated approach to migration policy to be taken at EU level. Although we on the Social Democratic side would have liked to see more redistribution between countries to ease the pressure on those who, like Sweden, have taken a great responsibility, the important thing about the proposal is that we get started and that we get things right in European migration policy. When we went into the process of drawing up a proposal for a new migration policy, we did so on the Social Democratic side with the aim of getting as broad a deal as possible. Unfortunately, we did not land in that, and I am the first to regret it. It is clear that it would have been much better for Sweden if we had landed in a united report, but now we landed in a report which nevertheless captures a great deal of what the discussions between the political parties touched upon. For us Social Democrats, there are a number of points that are important to bear in mind for the future migration policy and which have their starting point in the report. The first point is to protect the right of asylum. Every person has the right to flee and the right to have his or her case tried. Then we can deal with it in different ways at European level. We may have common rules for this, but we can never deny anyone the right to seek refuge from persecution and oppression. The second point that was important to us was to get legislation in place that does not take us back to the situation in 2015. This means that we must have a more even distribution in Europe and that Sweden cannot compensate for the lack of responsibility of other countries. This in turn means that Sweden cannot have a regulatory framework that essentially differs from that of other EU countries. The third point is that we must have a migration policy linked to integration policy. This means, for example, that in the same way as Sweden and a couple of other countries cannot take most of the responsibility, no municipalities can take full responsibility for integration. We all need to help each other. After much talk and threats from previous bourgeois governments on this issue, this was launched only when a Social Democratic minister took office after the 2014 elections, and if my memory does not completely fail me on this issue, it is the same minister who is on the list of speakers later on the agenda here. He was the one who put forward proposals that led us to a joint and several reception in Sweden, where all municipalities are allowed to take part in taking responsibility. The fourth point, and the last one I would like to mention, is that the examination of asylum is always an individual examination. It also means that there must always be legislation where there should be valves that allow for the unforeseen and counteract the obvious inhumane. But it also means that general amnesties are not something to be used, and it also means that generalising and sweeping prejudices should not be allowed to form the basis for assessing people's asylum grounds. We must never forget that it is people, individuals, with different reasons and with different lives that we must always take into account. It is our mission to take these considerations into account with us when we draft legislation on future migration policy. With this, Madam President, I am in favour of the Social Security Committee's proposal in the report and, therefore, of the Government's draft budget for Category 8.

i-d8f60b2d6de9cced-1151	Madam President, I would like I believe that it is impossible to cope with the major challenge we face in the area of migration without deepening European cooperation. East, south and in the Middle East region there is widespread disintegration. The combination of this means, on the one hand, that we have very large flows of refugees into Europe and, on the other hand, that what is happening in southern Europe also affects us in Sweden. In fact, we are both part of the responsibility for the situation and part of the solution to it. There are no isolated islands like that in Europe today. The most important issue is to bring about a workable, long-term sustainable regulatory framework at European level, and at Swedish level of course. But we cannot decouple our migration policy from what is happening at European level. How do you solve it? It is a difficult issue, given that there are so different views between the Member States in Europe, but in the end we must achieve something. Now the European Commission and the German Presidency are struggling to try to thresh through an asylum package. It has been going back and forth for a number of years. It is a question of border procedures and what the situation should be at the European Union's external border. And it is a question of being able to get a quick return for those who lack asylum reasons, which unfortunately is very many of those seeking asylum. At the same time, this must be done in such a way that the people who actually have asylum grounds are tried. It is a rather delicate task for the countries of the European Union, including Sweden, to be able to do this. In the longer term, it is crucial that we achieve stability in our environment. It is very difficult to achieve stability in countries that are in disintegration, in countries that have never seen democracy, in countries that are governed by heavy-handed dictatorships and, of course, in countries that have been characterised by wars and conflicts for decades. However, this arduous walk will ultimately mean that Europe needs to take greater responsibility for its neighbourhood in the form of broad agreements on development, investment and so on. I am a little worried that the European Union is focusing far too much on the readmission part of the dialogue and relations with third countries, for the reason that the countries will not be very keen to sign the agreements if that is the only thing they offer them. Instead, we must see that our continent – which is very rich, dynamic, growth-oriented and so on – will have to contribute more, and that will require deeper European cooperation. I think you're naive if you think something else. If we also look at the influx of refugees and humanitarian aid to them, we can see that the aid is disappearing weak. The year is almost over, but the UN appeal to help the Syrian refugees is only up to 35% – even though we are almost there on Christmas Eve. The appeal to the refugees in Venezuela, which is the second largest refugee disaster in the world right now and which has enormous consequences for Venezuela's South American neighbours, is 46 percent. The appeal for South Sudan is only 10 percent fulfilled, even though the year is coming to an end. The appeal for the Democratic Republic of Congo is only up to 7%, which is a little disappearing. The same applies to the Horn of Africa and Yemen – 34%, i.e. about one third of the need. As long as countries are not prepared to finance humanitarian international aid to refugees, there is clearly a risk of triggering further flows of refugees and so on. As a democratic, rich, free and prosperous country with functioning institutions, Sweden has a responsibility to stand up for people fleeing oppression and war. We have also taken a great deal of responsibility. If we look per person, we have taken a much greater responsibility than the vast majority of countries around us – than all the countries around us, I would actually say. And we're gonna keep running. We have a very ambitious quota refugee reception, and we have legislation that allows convention refugees and persons in need to converge rather than make a big distinction between these groups. However, we must also see that our reception will need to change some in the future. Like all countries, we have a welfare state to fund and integration to deal with. We have a situation in our country that means that a significant increase in the reception of asylum seekers and family immigrants to Sweden is at risk of causing many residential areas to have very difficult to keep their noses above water level. We see an increase in overcrowding and enormous difficulties for social services, schools, police and so on to cope with this. I am convinced that Sweden has the capacity to deal with the challenges we are facing, but we must understand that there is a contradiction between, on the one hand, having a very comprehensive reception, a lack of integration and a fairly highly regulated labour market and, on the other hand, perhaps having the world's most ambitious welfare state. How do we solve that, Madam President? Yes, we are getting a broad agreement on migration policy, as we have been trying to do for a year in the Migration Policy Committee. On the Liberal side, we broadly agreed with the proposal in the report, but we have two reservations, one of which concerns a specific legislative point relating to the supply requirements of family migration. Otherwise, we are completely in line, as they say, with the report's proposal. Then, of course, there are issues that are not dealt with in the report and where we have opinions. The Social Democrats have no reservation at all, and some parties in here have quite few reservations. There should be conditions for parties in this House to agree on a broad, long-term sustainable migration policy for Sweden – not only for the sake of politics and confidence in Parliament, but above all for those people who seek asylum and need to know what applies, as well as for our ability to be a long-term immigration-friendly country. Finally, Madam President, if we fail to achieve a long-term sustainable migration policy and fail to cope with exclusion in many areas of Sweden, I believe that this will further weaken the confidence of the people that we should have a generous and humane immigration policy. It will drive policy in a more polarising direction. It is becoming more conflict-filled, and there is increasing xenophobia and racism in response to the fact that policies are unable to cope with these challenges. That is why this is an extremely important issue for Sweden – and it is an important issue for the European Union as a whole.

An inhumane refugee policy that leads to unreasonable results for people will never be sustainable in the long term. The demands for changes will be as tight as the reports on how vulnerable people are being squeezed. Madam President, I would like People who have moved to Sweden for protection here must be given the best opportunity to establish a life here and become part of Sweden. A humane migration policy is about standing up for the right of asylum but also about giving those who have come here an opportunity to establish themselves. We are concerned that so many who advocate reduced reception of refugees distort the debate on establishment with very poorly substantiated arguments. If you say that the right to asylum should be conditional on how well our society manages to integrate people, you end up completely wrong. It will then be quite clear that what you want to get at is not really good integration, but the aim is to reduce immigration in itself. If we weaken the possibility of integration through a problematic migration policy, everything will be completely reversed. The Green Party is asking for a factual discussion on what efforts effectively lead to an independent life for those who have been granted protection from war and persecution and how we make them part of our common welfare building. As we see it, it is a matter of fast and legally secure handling and of early contacts with Swedish society, where the Swedish language is central. That is why we have been pushing for a long time to give the Migration Agency and the Migration Courts sufficient resources to handle the processing and to give asylum seekers the opportunity to learn the language from day one and have access to the labour market. An incredibly central part of this is that residence permits are something you can trust. Knowing what applies is fundamental when you are going to establish a new life here in Sweden. Another crucial factor for integration is the possibility of bringing the family together. Concern about their own future, as well as concern about a family that exists in a refugee camp or in a war situation, is a very bad prerequisite for focusing on education, on finding out about the labour market or on getting a business started and being able to make a living. Combating family reunification is therefore not only cynical but also a bad way of laying the foundations for good establishment and integration. In addition, we know that children who come to Sweden early have much better opportunities to get through school with sufficient results. That is another reason not to work to ensure that family reunification takes place late in the lives of children, but rather early. Madam President, I would like For good integration, we must also work hard on areas that are outside the migration policy itself. It is about the need for the establishment programme, the school, adult education, the SFI, housing policy and labour market policy to work together. Asylum seekers are not a homogeneous group. Therefore, we believe that the state needs to take greater responsibility for a more personalised establishment process. A special focus should be placed on the way to self-sufficiency for newly arrived women. In order to improve integration, we also need to increase social mobility in society. It is about adult education, reduced school segregation and better opportunities to enter the housing market and get a permanent home in Sweden. Madam President, I would like The Green Party has never hymned that the reception of refugees presents challenges in terms of introduction to the labour market, housing and good schooling. We also have clear proposals to improve this process. We believe that Sweden has the capacity to both protect the right to asylum and conduct a humane migration policy and an effective establishment policy.

It will be very clear, and we will come back to this in the forthcoming discussions. Sweden is today a country characterised by diversity. 20% of our population was born in another country. But what unites us is that we are all equal and that our common home is Sweden. Integration is precisely about getting all these different parts to function as a whole based on our fundamental values of democracy, human rights and gender equality. It is not always easy to do that, but it is possible, and the road to integration is called work. This autumn it is exactly five years since the major refugee crisis. In autumn 2015, Sweden received over 100 000 asylum seekers. Most of them came from war Syria. We then managed to give all the roofs over our heads and food for the day through a huge effort by the Migration Agency, the municipalities and civil society. That's good. But it was also obvious to most that Sweden would not be able to continue receiving at that rate. We had to stop in order to give all those who have already come a good reception and a good chance of integration. Then we had to introduce border controls on 12 November 2015. We had to introduce ID checks on 4 January 2016. We had to bring asylum legislation down to the EU minimum level with the temporary law that entered into force on 20 July 2016. I was the minister responsible at the time, too. It was not the simplest political task that we have had to perform, nor the most popular one. This was very controversial and very controversial. It was hardly, for example, that we got through the proposal on the ID checks in this House. For example, the Moderates were unable to vote in favour of the ID checks at that time. But we did, and it was absolutely necessary and we also gave results. Since 2015, the number of asylum seekers to Sweden has decreased by over 90%, and today we have the lowest level in 20 years. But not only the number of asylum seekers has decreased, but also the proportion that comes to Sweden has decreased. At most, Sweden received 12% of all asylum seekers who came to the EU. Today it is 3%, that is, about the proportion that Sweden has of the EU population and GDP. This gave us breathing space so that we could concentrate on integrating all those who had come and built homes, expanding schools and health care, creating fast-tracks into the labour market for the tens of thousands of new arrivals and at the same time rejecting and sending home those who have not been granted a residence permit. There were many who said at the time, five years ago, that we would never be able to do this, that the state finances would fail, that unemployment would fall and that social security benefits would fall to the ceiling. Today we know that none of it came true. On the contrary, during the last term of office, public debt fell by SEK 130 billion. During the last term of office, unemployment also fell by one fifth. During the last term of office, the rate of social security benefits fell by nearly 10 per cent. So five years ago, the accidents were wrong. The most important reason for this is that with our policy, more and more new arrivals came to work more and more quickly. I said in a previous line shift that during the alliance years the rate of integration was miserable. On average, it took almost ten years for half of the newcomers to enter the labour market. That's what happened. We almost halved that time down to 4 - 5 years. During the last parliamentary term, therefore, new arrivals came to work almost twice as fast as in the alliance years, despite the fact that we had received so many refugees. Actually, if you think about it, it's remarkable. It was good what we did then, but we can't do it again. We should not have to do it again, because it is not certain that we will be able to do it as well next time. I am fundamentally proud that since the war broke out in Syria in 2011, we have provided shelter for over 180 000 refugees from that war. It is men, women and children who might otherwise have perished. This is Sweden's greatest humanitarian effort ever. It beats everything else. I'm basically proud of it, as I said. But everyone will understand that it is not reasonable or sustainable if Sweden and some other countries continue to take disproportionate responsibility. That is why we should have a common European regulatory framework in which we help each other and in which we create order and order instead of the chaos that we experienced a few years ago. Such a regulatory framework is currently being developed under Ylva Johansson's leadership in the European Commission. But even more important is the recognition that one country cannot have asylum legislation that deviates significantly from that of the other EU countries. Future asylum legislation must be in line with that of other EU countries, but at the same time it must be humane, legally secure and contribute to greater integration. We have now received such a proposal from the Migration Committee. I think it looks basically good. This means a transition to temporary residence permits. But it is also based on a reasonable balance between rights and obligations. The right of asylum is upheld. The person in need of protection should be protected, first through temporary residence permits. But after three years you can apply for a permanent residence permit that you can get if you meet certain requirements: that you can support yourself, that you have basic knowledge of Swedish and how Swedish society works and that you have not committed any crimes. It contains rights and obligations – the right to protection but the obligation to do what you can to get into the new society, to integrate. It's clear, it's clear and it's easy for everyone to understand. These bills are for referral. We expect to come to Parliament this spring with the proposal, in order to have a new law in place on 20 July 2021. One can calculate with a crooked stick that the low-wage groups are much more likely to be exploited, especially when they come to a country that already has 450 000 unemployed. We have taken action against this. We've increased workplace inspections. We have doubled the fine imposed on rogue employers who employ workers who do not have a right of residence in the country. i-d8f60b2d6de9cced-1274	Madam President, I would like The basis for humanitarian protection is to avoid unreasonable situations as we move from permanent residence permits as a base to temporary residence permits. There must be a safety valve to deal with certain particularly distressing situations. Take, for example, the following situation: A 40-year-old has been here eight to ten years, works, pays taxes, has a family, knows Swedish and is integrated in Sweden, but has gone around on temporary residence permits. Suddenly there will be peace in the home country, and this person will be tested against the need for protection. Then the question is: If the need for protection has ceased, is he or she to be expelled? No, I do not think that is reasonable, because then the person has such a connection with Sweden that in that situation there must be an opportunity to obtain a residence permit, but not a permanent residence permit, because that is not what it is about, but an extension of the temporary residence permit. Then, of course, you will be able to work your way in and get a permanent residence permit if you meet the requirements that exist. It is an example of such a situation. This does not include those who have a final rejection. If it is in the bottom, as many of the unaccompanied people have, it is not covered by the humanitarian protection base. In addition, it is not only about the time you have been in Sweden, but you must also be able to show something else that gives you the particularly distressing circumstances that you should be able to refer to. Does this mean that no unaccompanied minors will be included? No, nor can it be said, since this is tested individually on a case-by-case basis. This is not an amnesty, as some claim, but a way of avoiding unreasonable situations for which a safety valve must be provided.

i-d8f60b2d6de9cced-1328	Madam President, I would like The authorities in the expenditure area have an important democratic role to play in analysing, forecasting and compiling information on state finances, on reforms and on the economy in general.
