The Swedish democrats are currently seeing a significant risk that a lack of funding from the agency will lead to an outdated regulatory framework that in the future does not keep up with the pace of innovation in the Swedish fintech sector. Many of the Swedish companies are at the forefront of the technology shift that is currently under way, and thus there is a high demand for Finansinspektionen's expertise and experience. The supply of financial services is increasing and is likely to continue at an unabated pace. Therefore, the Swedish Democrats see a need for an increase of SEK 5 million per year during the budget period, in accordance with the Authority's own request. Furthermore, Madam President, during the year we have seen major falls on the world's stock exchanges, and because of that we also saw a Swedish credit market froze. The reason for this was a lack of liquidity. Due to the large stock exchanges, many attempted to dispose of their holdings simultaneously in a market which was largely without buyers and thus unable to price the assets properly. In the long run, a malfunctioning credit market would also pose a risk to financial stability as companies become more dependent on bank financing. Bank financing is already the main source of finance for companies in a banking sector which, in relation to GDP, is by far one of the largest in the world. This is particularly serious because 35 of the total of 60 European funds temporarily closed down when the Swedish credit market froze were Swedish. This, of course, needs to be addressed. Then the question is how we do it. The answer of the Swedish democrats to that question is as follows: we must increase liquidity, and we must improve transparency. The basic problem in the credit market is that transparency is limited because much of the trading takes place outside regulated trading venues – so-called OTC trading or over-the-counter trading. There, information on closing prices is difficult to access, and information on trade volumes is usually non-transparent. However, at an international level, there are examples of systems where price, volume and party information is published only minutes after the transaction has taken place. In this way, it has also been possible to create a register where aggregate data on bond turnover can be built. In the United States, for example, this has increased transparency and improved transparency for investors and enabled them to analyse the liquidity of the underlying papers. The Swedish Democrats want to see a similar modernised and digitised system for bond trading in Sweden. Furthermore, Madam President, we have seen in the wake of the pandemic that Finansinspektionen has granted a temporary amortisation relief to the country's households at least until August 2021. This is, in our view, a completely correct decision, but at the same time it undermines the whole raison d'être of the second amortisation requirement because the second amortisation requirement was introduced on the grounds that it would increase resilience in times of crisis. Now we are in a time of crisis, but instead we need to temporarily abolish the requirement because, on the contrary, it has reduced the resilience of households during the pandemic – that is, contrary to the whole idea of origin. At best, the second amortisation requirement leads to the exclusion of households with good payment capacity from the housing market and to the shift to an uncertain secondary market with sky-high rents. It can be both single and young adults. This may, for example, involve a couple who have recently graduated from the university, with good cash flow but with a level of effort that activates the second amortisation requirement and thus makes it impossible to enter the housing market in our most rapidly growing and labour-intensive regions. Since the peak in 2017, residential building has decreased by 30 per cent in the kingdom and by as much as 50 per cent in the Stockholm region. In the worst case, the requirement is thus directly inhibiting growth and leads to socio-economic welfare losses. The government must immediately take political action to abolish the requirement because we are now black and white that the second amortisation requirement is ineffective and counterproductive. This is also a line that is supported in the research by Professor Lars EO Svensson, the Financial Policy Council and the Stockholm Chamber of Commerce. In conclusion, Madam President, I do not have a motion here today because the level of appropriations of the Swedish Democrats exceeds the expenditure framework laid down by Parliament. However, I have presented proposals that would ensure the financing of Finansinspektionen's innovation centre and thus improve the opportunities and prospects for Swedish fintech innovation. I have presented proposals that would increase liquidity and improve transparency in the Swedish credit market. I have also presented proposals that would enable more young adults to realise the dream of a home of their own.

Fossil assets are becoming increasingly risky to hold, and our common money, which we manage in the AP funds for this generation and for future generations, must be managed in a responsible manner that ensures that we do not endanger the climate and our own future livelihoods. That is why we are proposing a major human resources effort aimed at significantly improving the conditions for elderly workers. We need to earmark funds and demand compensation from the municipalities. We need to reduce the number of involuntary shared tours that make it difficult for many to bring together family life and working life, and which put a lot of stress on employees. We need to bring up the satisfaction and influence of the employees, and we need to ensure that managers in the care of the elderly have reasonable management responsibilities. Today, every manager in the care of the elderly has direct employee responsibility for an average of 60 people. We have been pointing this out for a long time and have presented reforms to overcome, but the government has been passive. Of course, it is not good enough for elderly care workers to become more or less managementless, unleaded, when they end up in difficult and vulnerable situations. It is clear that this, too, is an explanation for the high rates of sick leave that were already in the care of the elderly when we entered the pandemic. We also see a strong need to improve staff continuity and make sure that more people want to work in elderly care and that the elderly in need of home care can meet fewer people at home. It also significantly improves the quality of the elderly care that is carried out. We know that the building of relationships between the care provider and the care provider is very important. We spend a total of 4.4 billion more than the government on care for the elderly, and we spend some of that money on increasing medical skills in care for the elderly, adding more nurses, to a medical officer for rehabilitation in each municipality and removing the ban on employing doctors in the municipalities. We also spend more than the government on health care to ensure that when the spread of infection, this acute situation, settles and we start to deal with the health debt, we take all the capacity that exists in Sweden and coordinate it in a good way so that we can ensure that the citizens in need of care can get it no matter where that care is located in our country. This means 10 percent more than normal cost increase. It will have very big consequences and will in practice bring a very large number. The supply of personnel will be a huge challenge for the entire Swedish labour market for probably 25 years ahead, and this will affect municipalities very hard and clearly. Madam President, I would like The Center Party has made itself known as perhaps the main opponent of targeted state subsidies. I think we should highlight the trend break that I see. There has actually been a shift in systems in 2020. Now there will be more general state subsidies, or what we call whimsical contributions, which will be distributed according to a distribution key. There will be no more targeted state subsidies, but we have moved away from that system. It is true that, for historical reasons, perhaps 200 targeted state subsidies remain, but I see that there is a whole new system of thinking on the way. The basis is that targeted state subsidies drive huge administrative costs, counteract efficiency improvements and tie local decision-making. Here I think there has been a swing for the better, which I am very happy about. i-d8f60b2d6de9cced-1577	Madam President, I would like Quite exactly a year ago, I stood in this speaker's chair and debated last year's budget decision on the EU levy. I then expressed my concern and that of the Moderates for a sharp increase in the EU levy in the coming years. At that time, we did not know that there would not only be a sharp increase in Swedish contributions in 2021, but we would also be forced to participate in a comprehensive so-called recovery fund where we will put future generations into debt with borrowed funds in order to provide subsidies to European countries that have been mismanaging their economy for many years. The issue of the Swedish EU levy in the EU's future long-term budget has been discussed many times in both the Finance Committee and the EU Board, and I have been involved in most of these deliberations. On several occasions I and my moderate colleagues have asked for greater clarity from Swedish ministers and from the Prime Minister. On the contrary, however, we have seen an increasingly worrying picture in which representatives of the government, including the Prime Minister, have sometimes sounded a little well-described and signalled weakness to those EU countries that are pushing for a larger budget. It has been more or less clearly signalled that Sweden is unlikely to be able to negotiate a solution that is favourable to Sweden without, in practice, having to prepare for a substantial increase in the fee. From a moderate point of view, this is an unfortunate way of negotiating. One cannot back down into a negotiation but must be clear with one's line. Unfortunately, the negotiations did not go Sweden's way either. Although we were allowed to keep Swedish discounts on the EU levy, the fee is still increasing by a whole 16% – although an EU country is leaving the Union and the budget should therefore rather be reduced. And on top of it all, the future cost of the previously mentioned recovery fund is added. On the moderate side, we are disappointed by the outcome of the budgetary negotiations and, furthermore, are very critical of the European Recovery Fund, which was even bigger and more expensive than we feared. What are Sweden's costs for the EU per year to come? We were informed by the Minister of Finance at the meeting of the EU Board on 27 November. The usual long-term budget for 2021 – 2027 means that Sweden's annual fee rises from just under 39 billion per year to nearly 45 billion per year, an increase of 16 percent. Then the negotiated Swedish discounts on the fee are deducted. In the case of the so-called "Recovery Fund", the Next Generation EU, it is outside the EU's long-term budget. The full amount of this fund will be raised by the EU with the Member States as guarantors. The money is then distributed as grants and loans to the Member States, but mostly to southern and eastern Europe. The EU is postponing costs, and Member States are to start repaying the loan only in 2028 and continue for 30 years until 2058. For Sweden, the cost in today's monetary value is an extra 4.9 billion per year over these 30 years, or a total of 147 billion. And as usual, we are big net contributors; we pay a lot more than we get back. The paradox is that the EU has decided to call this debt on future generations for today's spending on the Next Generation EU. But the next generation inherited from us is the debt, which is to be repaid starting in 2028 and then 30 years later. Few of today's leading politicians will have to take responsibility for what happens from 2028 to 2058, and it is very practical to use money today that someone else will have to pay in the future. Just as strange is the use of the pandemic as an argument for borrowing this gigantic recovery fund. The money is not distributed at all according to the damage the pandemic has caused each country. Sweden has been hit harder than many other countries and has also among the higher death rates per capita in the EU. Nor do we seem economically to have fared much better than others. Despite this, we receive a very small share of the fund, and other countries that have suffered less from the pandemic receive much more. Madam President, I would like As the EU budget grows too much and the huge recovery fund is on top, the EU will have a total balance of approximately SEK 18 000 billion for the next budget period, 2021 – 2027. It's a huge amount of money. With such a large budget, it is of course particularly important to combat errors, cheating and corruption, and there the EU Court of Auditors has an important role to play. The latest review criticises the fact that the errors and deficiencies unfortunately increased in 2019. At the meeting of the EU Board on 27 November, I presented to the Minister for Finance the call for a sharper wording of Sweden's position on this criticism. We thought the government text was far too cautious and wanted to sharpen it properly. I appreciate the fact that the Minister of Finance agreed to our proposal for a sharper wording. Sweden is a major net contributor to the EU, and the new budget and the additional recovery fund mean that the EU has more of our Swedish tax money than ever before. It is even more important that money does not disappear through negligence or, at worst, through cheating or corruption. On point after point, it turns out that the Swedish Government cannot resist strong forces in the EU that push for decisions that disadvantage Sweden. Madam President, I would like Within the EU, there are now strong voices in favour of introducing what can in practice be called pure EU taxes. To put it in proportion, that is more than what the government is investing in the security of our old, that is, Category 11. To this cost will be added our part of the recovery fund. France receives enormous aid, followed by countries such as Germany, Spain and Italy. In addition to special agricultural aid, large parts of the EU budget are allocated to agriculture. Today it is so bad that every EUR 3 in the budget goes to agricultural aid. In the best of worlds it is seen that this huge support goes to sustainable agriculture and farmers who really care for good animal husbandry. But this is not the case – France, Italy and Spain have become accustomed to the large subsidies and are putting an end to a new agricultural policy. The result is that the largest agricultural aid is given to the industrial giants and not to family farms – 80% of the aid goes to 20% of the producers. A modern, reduced EU budget – and thus a reduced EU levy – can only happen if all Member States realise that the path we have taken is unsustainable. This is a crisis package of small unreal SEK 8 000 billion in addition to the regular long-term budget. i-d8f60b2d6de9cced-1698	Madam President, I would like Agenda 2030 is a UN programme aimed at achieving long-term and sustainable global development. The Agenda has been signed by all UN Member States. It is not binding, so that everyone has signed it does not mean that everyone will fight for it. When you read about Agenda 2030 in the media you can get the impression that it is only about climate and environment in the narrow sense, but Agenda 2030 is actually very broad. The 19 development goals are about peace, poverty reduction, stopping hunger, quality education, improving the environment, reducing CO2 emissions, increasing equality and equality, safe and cheap energy, economic growth, the need to develop industry, industry and infrastructure – as mentioned by the previous speaker – and reducing economic gaps, securing jobs and more. Sweden has long been at the forefront of most of these areas. The major exception is well the objective of the sea and marine resources, where the Baltic Sea still requires a great deal of action from the countries around it, including Sweden. Let me give you some examples of how good Sweden is: Sweden's emissions per capita of carbon dioxide are 33% below the EU average and 10% below the world average, including poor countries. We can thank hydroelectric power and nuclear power for more than 40 years. Another example is biodiversity. Very few species have disappeared from Sweden. The few species that have disappeared in our country, however, lived predominantly in broken deciduous forests at Sweden's absolute southern border. Great efforts are being made to support such species and their important environments. In terms of economic gaps, Sweden has the lowest share of low-income earners in the EU – 3.6 per cent of employees in Sweden are considered low-income earners. Unfortunately, the proportion is increasing, and that is worrying. I want to show in this way that Sweden is quite leading. Then you can rightly ask the question: should we put gunpowder on Agenda 2030 when we are already in such good shape? Our answer is: Yes, but calmly, and with our own current problems in mind. By its very nature, Agenda 2030 is organised from top to bottom. At the top are the UN and its organisations, which work not least on the aid-related parts of the agenda. At the level of the state Sweden, the government describes in its proposal how it wants to steer Sweden's work in line with the agenda's regulations. The government gives county boards the role of driving regions and municipalities. Madam President, I would like In my municipality, there are parties that talk about Agenda 2030 as a whole pervading the municipality's work. That makes me pretty daft. Agenda 2030 is not a Swedish constitution and should not be so. It is an international expression of will and a programme among others. Sweden's municipalities should be characterised by self-government with the aim of improving life for citizens in the short and long term. In my municipality current issues at the moment – besides of course to afford school, health care and care – are to maintain or close village schools, to increase the quality of school education, to make companies feel comfortable in the municipality so that we get jobs and tax money and to increase the share of locally produced food in the municipality's kitchen because we want to keep the regions open and rich in species. Other municipalities need to give priority to reducing crime and improving integration, which is of course also important in my municipality. All these objectives can be justified with Agenda 2030, but I would like to emphasise that that is not why we are working with them. The point I want to make is that the initiatives should come from below, where you best know what the greatest needs are. Each municipality has its greatest needs. Having said that, many of the municipalities' problems are also contained in Agenda 2030, and we can describe them in terms of the agenda – provided, however, that it does not make the work of the municipalities more difficult. The Government places a great deal of responsibility on the county boards to push through Agenda 2030 towards the municipalities. We think that leads to top control. The regional reform assumes that development work should lie on the regions rather than on the county boards. The county administrative boards are state-owned, but the regions are governed by regional elected representatives, as well as the municipalities by their elected representatives. Therefore, it may be reasonable for the regions and municipalities to carry out the work, but that the county boards have an important role to play in the follow-up to Agenda 2030. Madam President, I would like To go up the hierarchy again and look at the state's work on Agenda 2030, technological development, industry, business and trade are important tasks. Sweden must be a strong engineering nation. If we are to contribute to global sustainable growth, we need to train many more engineers who can develop technical solutions that can be used both here and in other countries. We must also create good conditions for investment in new technologies. The government's support for hydrogen-reduced iron through the Hybrid project is a very good example, but the government's ambivalent approach to the forest sector is a major cloud of concern. Sweden's aid must, to a greater extent than it is today, give priority to helping poor countries to start production that can give them access to the world market and thus sustainable economic development. The government is happy to talk about concrete targets for climate action, and that is a good thing. Unless the government lets the EU put a stop to Sweden's taxonomy for green investments, unless the government itself creates continued uncertainty for forest owners and unless the government itself sets up unreasonable calculations for what is net greenhouse gas emissions, I am fully convinced that we will meet the net zero target of 2045. But in Agenda 2030 there are also other goals that relate to other problems we have Sweden – sky high unemployment among young people, shootings on the open street, robbery of children around their schools, queue to prisons and a corona crisis that has not yet reached its culmination. Global Development Goal No. 11 is about sustainable cities. Sub-goal 11:1 proposes that all residents should live in a safe home by 2030. We have a lot to work with here. According to the police, Sweden has 60 vulnerable areas and 22 particularly vulnerable areas. We need very clear and timely targets here, Madam President. The government wants Sweden to be the international leader in implementing Agenda 2030. Sweden usually tries to focus on internationally high-profile issues. From the Swedish Democrats' side, however, we can actually consider handing over the leadership shirt to Denmark, Finland or Norway. They are right on our heels, but they have less acute social problems than we here at home in terms of corona, failed integration and high gang crime as well as shortcomings in school. If necessary, then we can confidently focus on being a good player in the cluster right behind our good neighbours, while focusing on our acute problems and on issues relating to education, development and the conditions for our business community in the future.

i-d8f60b2d6de9cced-1730	Madam President, I would like When the 2030 Agenda was adopted in 2015, it was an ambitious agreement for sustainable development. Large and far-reaching goals were set to be met in 15 years. By 2030, the world would eradicate hunger and poverty as well as combat climate change and save our seas. These were big words that we can see today did not lead to the action that is necessary to achieve the objectives, and it is time to walk the talk. The Left Party welcomes the Government's proposal on Sweden's implementation of Agenda 2030. It is good that Sweden has a clear plan for how to work towards achieving the goals, and with ten years left and grand goals we understand that it is necessary to have a clear way forward. The Left Party, on the other hand, believes that the level of ambition in the work on Agenda 2030 is too low. If Sweden is to remain at the forefront, we need much better tools and a higher level of ambition, while the government can clearly show that the agenda's objectives are moving in the right direction at national as well as international level. The government can do that difficult today. In Sweden, economic inequality is increasing. Most of the national environmental quality objectives are not achieved, and many also go in a negative direction, while violence and violations, which to a large extent affect women, are not reduced either. In the follow-up motion to the bill, the Left Party proposed that the follow-up to the 2030 Agenda should take place more frequently than proposed by the Government. We are pleased to have received a response and a consensus in the Riksdag on the need for more frequent reporting from the Government to the Riksdag. The follow-up to implementation is an important part of the work on Agenda 2030, as it allows states to be held accountable for implementing the commitments arising from the agenda. Through a more frequent reporting of results every two years, we will have a clearer path towards achieving our goals. Anything else is unthinkable. Implementation and follow-up also require that all sectors of society and stakeholders interact and are involved in problem formulations and solutions. Civil society actors are indispensable for the promotion of human rights, democracy and sustainable development. The Left Party therefore shares the proposal of the Agenda 2030 delegation in its final report on the bill that Sweden needs an Agenda 2030 Forum. The proposal means that the Government organises a national forum to anchor the reporting and acquire knowledge from different sectors and actors before the occasions when Sweden submits in-depth national reporting to the UN High Level Forum. The Left Party also believes that we need an actor who has the necessary overall perspective for us to manage the implementation. At present, there is no such thing. In its response, the Agenda 2030 delegation proposes a committee tasked with continuously producing information to the government by 2030 in the form of follow-up, evaluation and proposals for measures. The Left Party shares the delegation's view that such a committee is needed. The need for a holistic approach is also evident when one talks about the agenda's target conflicts, but also the political and societal conflicts with the agenda. Although 17 years have passed since the PGU was adopted, and at the same time the Riksdag stated that Swedish policy must be coherent, it is clear that Swedish development policy is still not exactly that. The major conflicts and obstacles remain. Examples include arms exports, EU agricultural policy and tax evasion. During its term of office, the Government has taken or announced political decisions that go against the objectives of the PGU. The unwillingness to support country-by-country reporting at EU level, the agreement with Turkey to stop people fleeing, the tightening of Swedish asylum laws, export efforts aimed at dictatorships, the sale of Vattenfall coal mines and the inadequate legislation to restrict arms exports are some of the problems. The Agenda is not just about global development policy, but the commitments contained in the Action Plan also apply at national level. In Swedish domestic policy there are today major target conflicts with a negative impact on the implementation of Agenda 2030. These include tax reductions for the well-off who undermine welfare and increase inequality, which Oxfam in a new report also showed is a significant factor in driving further climate change. It is about the conflict of objectives between continued growth, how we achieve a prosperous society after 2030 and what we need to do in order to cope with the climate. It is also about the objective of sustainable consumption and production patterns and the change in labour law in relation to the Agenda's objective of decent working conditions for all, to name a few. The Left Party notes that despite the fact that Agenda 2030, with its 17 global goals for sustainable development, has gender equality both as its own goal and as a perspective that will permeate the whole agenda, a feminist analysis of the Government's proposal for Sweden's implementation is lacking. Women's rights are a crucial key to development. It is crucial that women in poor countries are allowed to put the agenda for work on gender equality in development policy and that Sweden works to ensure that women are represented and included in all forms of decision-making. Violations of women's rights are the most common and widespread violation of human rights. The Left Party therefore believes that the government, which claims to be feminist, should come back with a feminist analysis of the implementation of Agenda 2030. The Left Party notes that Agenda 2030, with its 17 objectives and 169 sub-targets, is not a buffet where it is possible to choose to implement the measures needed to achieve the objectives that are most easily achieved. To start picking the low-hanging fruits is well enough, but eventually Sweden also needs to deal with the big questions beyond the words. Sweden, as mentioned earlier, does not even reach its own set environmental targets. They are objectives that, if we reach them, would make it possible for us to reach more or less the 2030 Agenda targets on climate, the environment and water. With regard to the climate, economic inequality, job security, people living in exclusion and good education for all, we are not moving in the right direction. Sweden has been a leading country on these issues. The government and the Riksdag need to keep up this work to ensure that it continues to be so. The 2030 Agenda highlights what some see as a cliché, but in this case it is so true: Only together – all citizens, all parts of society and ultimately all nations – can we go to the goal of Agenda 2030. Then let's do it. I request the approval of reservation 4.

The Lancet's latest annual report on the impact of climate change on global health shows a bleak future. Scientists warn that if we do not stop climate change now, we will see more food shortages, heat-related mortality and increased incidence of infections, waterborne diseases, storms and fires. The pandemic has, of course, temporarily reduced climate emissions, but there are other effects of the pandemic that are worrying and that are at risk of lasting adverse health effects and increased poverty. This week Save the Children reported that many years of work against malnutrition is at risk of being lost as food production has decreased during the pandemic and food prices have been pushed up. School closures have meant that many children have lost not only the valuable schooling but, in poor countries, perhaps their only meal a day. The acute hunger is estimated to double this year due to the pandemic, to affect 265 million people. This year's Nobel Prize winner World Food Programme is needed more than ever. The World Bank estimates that between 88 and 115 million people will end up in extreme poverty in 2020, and the figure continues to rise to 150 million in 2021. It's a trend break in the wrong direction. Over the last twenty years, poverty has steadily decreased, but now it is increasing. In addition to more people being forced to live in extreme poverty, the consequence is that we are also backing down in other areas. Save the Children warns that more children end up in child labour and marriage when families are forced to find different ways to make a living. The IMF warns that the pandemic can add to conflicts and social unrest as competition increases for resources. They show that past pandemics have created a vicious spiral of economic distress, increasing inequality and social unrest in the most affected countries. Besides creating human suffering, growing poverty also affects us. The World Bank estimates that eight out of ten of those now slipping back to extreme poverty live in middle-income countries. These countries are important parts of global value chains and will be able to have a major impact on Western economies as well. i-d8f60b2d6de9cced-1784	Madam President, I would like "Sustainable development" is a development that meets today's needs without jeopardizing the potential of future generations to meet their needs. This was formulated in 1987 in the report "Our Common Future", the so-called Brundtland Report. Under the leadership of Norway's then Social Democratic Prime Minister Gro Harlem Brundtland, the Brundtland Commission had been commissioned by the UN to prepare proposals for a better environment and sustainable development. The report became an important starting point in the global work for sustainability. Not least, the basis for what was to be the next step was laid: the adoption of Agenda 21 by the United Nations at the Rio de Janeiro Conference in 1992, in which the world, with a view to the 21st century, would tackle environmental degradation and the fight against poverty and strengthen democracy. The ambitions of Agenda 21 were extensive. The idea was that it would not only be a desk product but would become a work that characterized the whole of society. Sweden took the work very seriously. The commitment was evident with the government, in the municipalities and out among associations. During my own schooling in the 1990s, we learned about Agenda 21. We were asked to consider how we could contribute ourselves, and the teaching was characterized by getting to know the world and taking responsibility for it. It was simply a solid public education effort for democracy and sustainability. The UN subsequently also adopted the so-called Millennium Goals. The aim was to eradicate poverty and hunger by 2015 and strengthen education and health with a particular focus on children. Now that we are about to implement Agenda 2030, we are taking on a job that people have done very ambitiously and meritoriously ahead of us. With Agenda 2030 we grab the baton for the next stretch. Sustainability has long been defined according to three dimensions: environmental sustainability, social sustainability and economic sustainability. The overall objective of Agenda 2030, now adopted by the Riksdag, includes this. We say that Sweden will implement the 2030 Agenda for economically, socially and environmentally sustainable development through a coherent policy at national and international level. We also assess that Sweden should carry out work that contributes to sustainable development in society nationally and internationally. The balanced and integrated approach to the economic, social and environmental dimensions of sustainability is central. This is good and right. There is also reason to think a little more deeply about the goal and the assessments. It is not straightforward to determine what is sustainable. What we are now committed to with Agenda 2030 is to analyse and evaluate the policies we are pursuing from the point of view of sustainability. As a guide, there are 17 objectives and 169 milestones. Each country decides on the basis of its own conditions how the objectives are to be achieved, and each country has the task of ensuring that all the different policies are consistent. Agenda 2030 also becomes a tool for identifying when they do not match, and it is a tool for identifying when different goals conflict with each other. The fact that there are goals conflicts is nothing unusual and nothing that can be completely avoided. Target conflicts are a natural part of political everyday life. Our task is to see them and understand them from all the essential sustainability angles and then make a political assessment of what is the most reasonable decision in the individual case and what boils together to the best whole. An important task is to achieve a broad social commitment behind Agenda 2030. This is not supposed to be an expert project that spins above people's heads, but vice versa. So we need to consider how we are also making sure that students are involved this time. We must help create pressure behind the international trade union work on human rights. We must evaluate what democratic arenas we have in which social debate can take place on equal and fair terms. Success in getting community involvement in the issues is important for our overall ability to make good analyses. We are unlikely to always agree on how things should be seen or what balance is right. Nor is that always the purpose. On the other hand, the method, transparency and commitment can mean that more people have the opportunity to opinion and influence and that decisions taken can gain greater legitimacy. A not insignificant UN ambition here is the higher democratic aim. A society governed by democratic principles and not only by the interests of the rich and the strong will be fairer and more peaceful. Democratising the world means being able to resolve conflicts and create improvements. This is also where international cooperation comes in to strengthen a democratic world order and to achieve the objectives of the agenda. Madam President, I would like Developing society for the better requires that we do not let dystopin eat us. It is important that we build faith in the future, that we believe in man's will and ability, and that we look optimistically at technological development and how it can help us. With the right policies, we can move in the right direction. There are several important examples of this in Sweden. Some examples are the Hybrid project in Luleå, where steel will now be produced without coal, LKAB's investment in Kiruna in fossil-free production and of course Northvolt in Skellefteå, this huge factory that will produce batteries for the growing market. This is about highly productive, competitive and green jobs that create growth, develop the economy, give tax money to care and contribute to the transition. This is how we combine sustainability aspects. To be successful in this classic Swedish structural change policy requires investments for new jobs. It also requires a generous unemployment fund, a broad labour market policy and plenty of training places, so that people can take the new jobs – because it is the people we work with. It is directly counter-productive to society's ability if some can cope with luxury consumption while others are forced to stress between insecure low-wage jobs. Justice is a prerequisite for the success of Agenda 2030. I myself am convinced that it will be fine. Sweden has several exemplary examples to point out from both yesterday and today. Now it is important that there is even more workshop. With this in mind, I would like to support the proposal in the report and reject the reservations. I would also like to wish you a Merry Christmas and thank you very much.
i-d8f60b2d6de9cced-1801	Madam President, I would like Building a better world often feels difficult. This year hasn't made it any easier. When the pandemic spread around the world, it was not that the problems and challenges we had before disappeared. Rather, they have increased. 2020 is coming to an end. There are many of us who hope that next year will bring more sparkles of light than this year has offered. Now developments in many parts of the world are going backwards. People are forced to leave their homes in the wake of climate change when impregnable areas of forest burn up and heavy rains and floods leave the ground unusable. In addition, a deadly virus is spreading, not only taking people's lives but also putting millions of people in acute and extreme poverty. Building a better world often feels difficult. But it's a little easier when we do it together. With Agenda 2030, the whole world has agreed on a concerted effort to achieve the 17 goals, which in itself will enable us to achieve social, environmental and economic sustainability. We must do so despite the setbacks. Now, instead, it is more important than ever to stick together and switch up. Therefore, it is also so good that this bill is now in place, so that our work, Sweden, can be focused and the goals achieved. The Agenda is an action plan with objectives for the transition to a sustainable society for people, the planet and prosperity. The world's countries are committed to leading the world towards a sustainable and just future by 2030, to eradicating poverty and hunger everywhere, to combating inequality within and between countries, to building peaceful, fair and inclusive societies, to ensuring that everyone is able to enjoy human rights, to promote equality and the empowerment of women and girls, to take efforts to reach those at risk and to ensure lasting protection for the planet and its resources. The fact that no one should be left out is a fundamental principle based on a rights perspective for the individual. Reducing inequalities both within and between countries is a prerequisite for giving all people access to basic public services such as housing, good health care, education and economic empowerment. Human and civil rights must be respected, and democracy is an invaluable tool. Madam President, I would like Building a better world often feels difficult. But starting where you stand is often a good start, and we will live up to the commitments we have at home. That is why I am concerned about the fact that Sweden's work on implementing Agenda 2030 is going much too slow. Last year, the Static Central Bureau produced a situational picture of how the work is progressing, and the results of it are nothing we can be satisfied with. It is noted, among other things, that our current instruments are not enough to meet environmental and climate objectives, that inequality is increasing rather than reducing, and that there is no reduction in violence and violations. It is absolutely crucial that our work on the three points should shift and take shape in the policy that we in this House decide. If we are to achieve the objectives of Agenda 2030, it is not possible to lower taxes for the rich or to make it cheaper to release; we have to do the opposite. But we don't just have our own goal fulfillment to think about. Sweden also has a responsibility and a great opportunity to help more people achieve their goals. Sweden's work in the world will also be permeated by the overall idea of Agenda 2030. In the footsteps of the coronapndemin, the work is in danger of sounding off and being completely replaced by emergency measures. But in order to create long-term work, it is important that the pandemic does not become a brake block but rather a reason to work more, harder and wider. It is important to restart with new and better conditions to meet the challenges of the future. A green reboot. Before the pandemic, in September 2019, a group of independent scientists appointed by the UN presented a progress report on the world's implementation of Agenda 2030. The report shows a slowdown in the pace of progress and that the world's countries were already a long way from achieving the targets. At the same time, the report shows that there is an opportunity to reverse this trend. Then we'll have to tap up. Sweden will continue to be a driving force in the EU and in international bodies for ambitious global implementation. This will be done in several ways, such as through trade, international cooperation, knowledge exchange, higher education, research and innovation. Sweden contributes through the participation of public, business, universities, colleges and civil society in several arenas, both bilaterally and multilaterally. We have a feminist government that pursues a feminist foreign and development policy. We do this because it really makes a difference in the lives of millions of women and girls. But we are also doing so because by strengthening women, economically, with more education, with more power over our own bodies, we are also getting closer to achieving the other goals, and not just that of equality. The same applies to the climate challenge. We do not have many years if we are serious about tackling the climate crisis. We get worrying reports every week now that things are going too slowly and that we need to do more. For economic development to take people out of poverty, there must also be a world in which to live. There are no jobs on a dead planet. Madam President, I would like Working inclusively and comprehensively can be difficult sometimes in our systems, which are largely built as drain pipes where each area is handled separately. Now we have to learn to work differently. We in the Green Party have always argued that different policies are interrelated and much more interdependent than is apparent at first sight. That's what the world's seen now, too. The Agenda is also about promoting and making visible the importance of coordination and coherence between all policy areas, both globally and nationally. The proposal put forward by the government underlines this. It is about how we take important steps in the management of processes in government, what is the role of the authorities and that we need to be clearer in procurement. The goal should also be parliamentary. That is why we will have to stand here in the late evening and debate this in order to anchor and clarify Sweden's commitment to implementing the 2030 Agenda both nationally and internationally. The Agenda extends to 2030, but work on sustainable development needs to continue beyond that, especially after the challenges of recent years and backlash. The fact that the world has common goals makes it easier. Now we're just going to achieve them, and we can only do that together. I therefore call for the committee's proposal to be approved in the report.
